1_{G}\colon\Pi_{1}\left(\hat{s}_{1},V\right)\rightarrow\bullet\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}G
denotes the constant functor sending every morphism to the trivial group element and
g=(g_{p})_{p\in P}
is a family of group elements
g_{p}\in G
, or, put differently, a family of constant functions
V(p)\rightarrow G
.

First, for a subset
S\subset P
of the plaquettes, let

\displaystyle W(S):=\{p\in P\,|\,V(p)\cap V(s)\neq\emptyset\text{ for some }s\in S\}\subset P,

and, moreover, set
W^{k}(S)=W(W^{k-1}(S))
. Choose a plaquette
p_{0}\in P
and a group element
g_{p_{0}}\in G
arbitrarily. Since
\Gamma
is a finite graph and its dual graph
\Gamma^{*}
is always connected, it holds that
W^{m}(p_{0})=P
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
, since sharing an edge for two plaquettes implies them having a common vertex. We define the family
g
recursively: For every
q\in W^{k+1}(p_{0})\setminus W^{k}(p_{0})
, choose a neighboring plaquette
p\in W^{k}(p_{0})
and set
g_{q}=g_{p}h_{p}(v)^{-1}h_{q}(v)
, where
v
is a vertex shared by
p
and
q
. Due to
W^{m}(p_{0})=P
, this defines a full family
g=(g_{p})_{p\in P}
. Once we have confirmed well-definedness of the assignment, we would like to set
\gamma(w):=g_{p}h_{p}(w)^{-1}
, for which we have to make sure that
g_{p^{\prime}}h_{p^{\prime}}(w)^{-1}=g_{p}h_{p}(w)^{-1}
whenever
p
and
p^{\prime}
share the vertex
w
. This boils down to checking the following two cases:

•

Suppose that well-definedness for
W^{k}(p_{0})
has already been verified and that the two group elements assigned to two elements
p,p^{\prime}\in W^{k}(p_{0})
induce the one assigned to
q
, where the notation is as above. This means that
q
shares a vertex
v
with
p
and a vertex
v^{\prime}
with
p^{\prime}
. Assuming the relation
g_{q}=g_{p}h_{p}(v)^{-1}h_{q}(v)
, we define a path
\delta\colon[0,1]\rightarrow\cup_{t\in N^{k}(p_{0})}\mathrm{cl}(t)
with
\delta(0)=v,\delta(1)=v^{\prime}
such that
g_{q}=g_{p^{\prime}}h_{p^{\prime}}(v^{\prime})^{-1}A([\delta])h_{q}(v)
:

Choose a sequence of plaquettes
p=p_{0},\ldots,p_{\ell}=p^{\prime}
, forming a path in the connected graph
\Gamma^{*}
with
p_{i}
and
p_{i+1}
sharing the vertex
v_{i+1}
, and choose paths
\delta_{i}\colon[0,1]\rightarrow\hat{p_{i}}
starting at
v_{i}
and ending at
v_{i+1}
. Here, our notation is such that
v_{0}=v
and
v_{\ell+1}=v^{\prime}
. Since
A([\delta_{i}])h_{p_{i}}(v_{i})=h_{p_{i}}(v_{i+1})
, the relations imply that setting
\delta=\delta_{\ell}\star\ldots\star\delta_{0}
gives the desired path. As
\pi_{1}\left(\hat{s}_{1}\right)
is trivial, the path
\delta
is homotopy equivalent relative to its endpoints to a path in
\mathrm{cl}(q)
that lifts to a path
\sigma
with
\mathrm{im}(\sigma)\subset\hat{q}
and
A([\sigma])h_{q}(v)=h_{q}(v^{\prime})
. We find

\displaystyle g_{q}=g_{p^{\prime}}h_{p^{\prime}}(v^{\prime})^{-1}A([\delta])h_{q}(v)=g_{p^{\prime}}h_{p^{\prime}}(v^{\prime})^{-1}A([\sigma])h_{q}(v)=g_{p^{\prime}}h_{p^{\prime}}(v^{\prime})^{-1}h_{q}(v^{\prime}),

showing that both
p
and
p^{\prime}
induce the same group element for
q
.

•

If
p
and
p^{\prime}
induce the group elements assigned to two neighboring plaquettes
q
and
q^{\prime}
sharing a vertex
v
, one needs to show the consistency relation
g_{q^{\prime}}=g_{q}h_{q}(v)^{-1}h_{q^{\prime}}(v)
. This is done similarly to the first case.

Now, with the above definition of
\gamma
, we also have the last remaining relation, namely

\displaystyle\gamma(v^{\prime})A([\tau])\gamma(v)^{-1}=g_{p}h_{p}(v^{\prime})^{-1}A([\tau])h_{p}(v)g_{p}^{-1}=1_{G}

for any path
\tau\colon[0,1]\rightarrow\hat{s}_{1}
from
v
to
v^{\prime}
. This proves that indeed,
(A,h)\cong(1_{G},g)
, which says that every gauge configuration is equivalent to one that is constant on a plaquette. However, two such objects
(1_{G},g)
and
(1_{G},\widetilde{g})
may be isomorphic. That is the case if and only if
g_{p}(\widetilde{g}_{p})^{-1}=g_{p^{\prime}}(\widetilde{g}_{p^{\prime}})^{-1}
, that is,
g,\widetilde{g}\in\mathcal{O}
for some orbit
\mathcal{O}\in G^{P}/G
. This proves
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}\simeq G^{P}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}G
.

Let now
\Gamma^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}
denote the graph labeled in
\vect_{\C}^{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}}
that defines the natural transformation
\mu_{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}
(cf.
Section

2.4.4
). Since
\mathbb{S}^{3}
is closed, Example 7.8 from
[
FMM
]
applies and
\mu_{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}\colon\C\Rightarrow\C
corresponds to a map
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}\rightarrow\C,h\mapsto\mu_{h}^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}
, where
h=(h_{p})_{p\in P}\in G^{P}
, which is constant on isomorphism classes. The invariant can then be expressed as

\displaystyle Z_{G}(B_{\Gamma})=Z_{DW}(\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma})=\sum_{[h]\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}})}\frac{\mu_{h}^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}}{|\Aut(h)|}=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{h\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}G^{P}}\mu_{h}^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}.

(4.5)

We want to relate the labeling of the
\vect_{G}
-labeled graph
\Gamma
to the one of the graph
\Gamma^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}
. For an edge
e\in E(\Gamma)
, recall the functors

\displaystyle P_{e}^{s}\colon G^{P}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}G\simeq\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}\xrightarrow{P_{v}}\mathcal{D}_{v}\xrightarrow{D_{s(e)}}\mathcal{D}_{e}=G\times G\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}G,

and
P_{e}^{t}=D_{t(e)}\circ P_{v^{\prime}}
, where
s(e)\colon v\rightarrow e
and
t(e)\colon v^{\prime}\rightarrow e
are the local strata defined by the vertices
v,v^{\prime}
where
e
starts and ends. In general, the functor
P_{v}\colon\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}\rightarrow\cald_{v}
maps
(A,h)
to
(h_{p}(v))_{p\in P(v)}
. Using the above equivalences replaces every gauge configuration by one constant on plaquettes, and so both functors
P_{e}^{s},P_{e}^{t}
are simply given as the obvious projections: A (constant) plaquette labeling
(h_{p})_{p\in P}\in G^{P}
maps to the pair of group elements in
\cald_{e}
given by the element
h_{\ell(e)}
residing on the plaquette on the left of a given edge
e
(with respect to the orientation of
e
and the plaquettes) and the group element
h_{r(e)}
on the right plaquette. That is, both functors act as

\displaystyle G^{P}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}G\rightarrow G\times G\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}G,(h_{p})_{p\in P}\mapsto\left(h_{\ell(e)},h_{r(e)}\right),

and we write
P^{e}:=P_{e}^{s}=P_{e}^{t}
. Similarly, we see that the natural transformation
\rho_{e}P_{e}^{d}\colon\rho_{e}\circ P_{e}^{s}\Rightarrow\rho_{e}\circ P_{e}^{t}
labeling the new bivalent vertices
v_{e}
of
\Gamma^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}
corresponds to the identity transformation. Thus, these vertices do not contribute to the scalar value of the graph as an endomorphism in the symmetric monoidal category
\vect_{\C}^{G^{P}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}G}
, and we can simply ignore the additional bivalent vertices in the remainder of the proof. Recall that to a vertex
v\in V(\Gamma)\subset V(\Gamma^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}})
, the natural transformation
\mu_{v}=\sigma_{v}P_{v}\colon\rho_{v}^{t}\circ P_{v}\Rightarrow\rho_{v}^{s}\circ P_{v}
is associated, where
\sigma_{v}
is obtained from a morphism
\alpha_{v}\in\vect_{G}\left(\C,\otimes_{e\in E(v)}V^{\varepsilon(e)}\right)
as in the proof of
Lemma

4.11
.
Each
\alpha_{v}
can be identified with an element of its target and may be decomposed as

\displaystyle\alpha_{v}=\sum_{g\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}\Omega_{v}}\alpha_{g}^{v}\in\bigoplus_{g\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}\Omega_{v}}\bigotimes_{e\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}E(v)}\left(V^{\varepsilon(e)}\right)_{g_{e}^{\varepsilon(e)}}\subset\bigotimes_{e\in E(v)}V^{\varepsilon(e)},

where we used the notation from
Lemma

4.11
. Similar to the subsets
\Omega_{v}\subset G^{E(v)}
, we define

\displaystyle\Omega=\Bigg\{(g_{e})_{e\in E}\in G^{E}\,\Bigg|\prod_{e\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}E(v)}g_{e}^{\mspace{1mu}\varepsilon(v,e)}=1_{G}\text{ for all }v\in V\Bigg\}\subset G^{E},

which we can view as the set of ‘admissible’ edge colorings, and we have an obvious family of projections
\pi_{v}^{E}\colon\Omega\rightarrow\Omega_{v}
. This allows us to decompose
\langle\Gamma\rangle=\sum_{g\in\Omega}\langle\Gamma\rangle_{g}
, where the components
\langle\Gamma\rangle_{g}
are built from the components
\alpha_{\pi_{v}^{E}(g)}^{v}
by tensoring and composition. The projection
\pi\colon G\times G\rightarrow G,(g,h)\mapsto h^{-1}g
and the functor
P^{e}
induce a map

\displaystyle\varphi\colon G^{P}\rightarrow\Omega,h=(h_{p})_{p\in P}\mapsto\left((\pi\circ P^{e})(h)\right)_{e\in E}=\left(h_{r(e)}^{-1}h_{\ell(e)}\right)_{e\in E}.

We claim that this is a well-defined and surjective map, such that for any
g\in\Omega
, the preimage is the orbit of some
h\in G^{P}
under the left
G
-action, that is
\varphi^{-1}(g)=Gh
. In fact, we have:

•

We check that
\varphi
is well-defined. Let
h\in G^{P}
be a plaquette labeling. We need to show that
\prod_{e\in E(v)}\varphi(h)_{e}^{\varepsilon(e)}=1_{G}
for every
v\in V(\Gamma)
. Assume that
e,e^{\prime}\in E(v)
are two neighboring edges, where
e
follows
e^{\prime}
in the cyclic order of
E(v)
. Then, in the case that, for instance, both edges point toward
v
, it holds that
h_{r(e^{\prime})}=h_{\ell(e)}
and if e.g. only
e^{\prime}
points outwards at
v
, then
h_{\ell(e)}=h_{\ell(e^{\prime})}
. In the first case, the factor
\varphi(h)_{e^{\prime}}^{-1}\varphi(h)_{e}^{-1}
occurring in the product evaluates to
h_{\ell(e^{\prime})}^{-1}h_{r(e)}
, in the second case, the factor
\varphi(h)_{e^{\prime}}\varphi(h)_{e}^{-1}
contracts to
h_{r(e^{\prime})}^{-1}h_{r(e)}
. The remaining cases are similar. Moving once around the vertex according to the cyclic order of
E(v)
, we see that the above product is trivial, so
\varphi
maps to
\Omega
.

•

We prove surjectivity. Let
g\in\Omega
be an admissible edge coloring. We consider the dual graph
\Gamma^{*}
, where the vertices are the plaquettes, that is
V(\Gamma^{*})=P
, and the edges are perpendicular to the edges
E
, but in one-to-one correspondence. We define an orientation on the dual edges so that
e
points from
r(e)
to
\ell(e)
. The defining conditions for
\Omega
now translate into the requirement that the oriented product of the group elements
g_{e}
(or their inverses) associated with the edges in every loop of
\Gamma^{*}
be trivial. Since
\Gamma^{*}
is connected (even though
\Gamma
does not have to be), we can choose a spanning tree
T\subset\Gamma^{*}
. We select a starting vertex
p_{0}\in V(T)
and a group element
h_{p_{0}}
and color, starting from
p_{0}
, the remaining vertices in
T
by the rule that
h_{p^{\prime}}=h_{p}g_{e}
for
e\in E(T)
an edge pointing from
p
to
p^{\prime}
. Now, if this relation holds for all edges, then
h=(h_{p})_{p\in P}\in G^{P}
defines a plaquette labeling such that
\varphi(h)=g
. For this, we have to check that the relation above holds if an edge
e\in E(\Gamma^{*})\setminus E(T)
not contained in
T
connects two dual vertices
p
and
p^{\prime}
. Due to the properties of a spanning tree, we can always find a path from
p^{\prime}
to
p
by connecting edges
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}\in E(T)
, which closes to a loop by adding the edge
e
. Then, it holds that
g_{e_{1}}^{\varepsilon(e_{1})}\cdot\ldots\cdot g_{e_{k}}^{\varepsilon(e_{k})}\cdot g_{e}=1_{G}
, where the signs are evaluated with respect to the vertices of
\Gamma
on the left of the loop (with respect to the orientation on the boundary). But this directly implies that
h_{p^{\prime}}=h_{p}g_{e}
.

•

For the second claim, assuming that
\varphi(h)=\varphi(h^{\prime})
for
h,h^{\prime}\in G^{P}
implies for any two neighboring plaquettes, and by connectivity of
\Gamma^{*}
for all plaquettes
p
and
p^{\prime}
, that
h_{p}(h^{\prime}_{p})^{-1}=h_{p^{\prime}}(h^{\prime}_{p^{\prime}})^{-1}
. From that, it immediately follows that
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}
for some orbit
\calo\in G^{P}/G
, so
\varphi^{-1}(\varphi(h))=\mathcal{O}=Gh
. Surjectivity of
\varphi
implies the claim.

Hence
\varphi
induces a bijection
\overline{\varphi}\colon G^{P}/G\rightarrow\Omega
from the orbit space to the set of edge colorings. We can now conclude, using that
G
acts freely on
G^{P}
, that

\displaystyle\langle\Gamma\rangle=\sum_{g\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}\Omega}\langle\Gamma\rangle_{g}=\sum_{\calo\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}G^{P}/G}\langle\Gamma\rangle_{\overline{\varphi}(\calo)}=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{h\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}G^{P}}\langle\Gamma\rangle_{\varphi(h)}=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{h\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}G^{P}}\mu_{h}^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}.

For the last equality
\langle\Gamma\rangle_{\varphi(h)}=\mu_{h}^{\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}
, we have used that the left-hand side arises by composing morphisms
\alpha_{\pi_{v}^{E}(\varphi(h))}^{v}=(\widetilde{\sigma}_{v})_{P_{v}(h)}
(cf. the proof of
Lemma

4.11
),
the right-hand side arises by composing morphisms
(\mu_{v})_{h}=(\sigma_{v})_{P_{v}(h)}
and the underlying diagram representing this morphism in
\vect_{\C}
can be transformed into
\Gamma
by isotopy and using the symmetric braiding, mapping
\sigma_{v}
to
\widetilde{\sigma}_{v}
. Together with
Eq.

4.5
, we get
Z_{G}(B_{\Gamma})=\langle\Gamma\rangle
, finishing the proof.
∎

The final property we have to show is
3
.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
M\in\Cob_{3}^{\vect_{G}}(\emptyset,\emptyset)
be a defect cobordism to which the tube-capping move can be applied with respect to an embedding of an oriented cylinder. Denote by
V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}\in\vect_{G}
the objects labeling the defect edges on the cylinder and let further
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
be a basis of
\vect_{G}\left(V_{1}^{\varepsilon(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{n}^{\varepsilon(n)},\C\right)
. Then,
Z_{G}(M)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}Z_{G}\left(\Delta_{3}^{\alpha_{i}}(M)\right)
holds for Dijkgraaf-Witten theory
Z_{G}
.

Proof.

As in the proof of
Proposition

3.18
, we can assume that all signs are positive. We adopt the notation
V
for the family of
G
-graded vector spaces
\{V_{i}\}
and
\alpha
for the basis
\{\alpha_{i}\}
given in the proposition. Under the identification from
Eq.

2.1
,
we denote by
\alpha_{i}^{*}\in\vect_{G}\left(\C,V_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{n}\right)
the elements of the dual basis
\alpha^{*}
.

As usual, it suffices to consider the situation locally around the cylindrical region to which
\Delta_{3}^{\alpha_{i}}
is applied. On the one hand, as in
Proposition

3.18
, we have the cylinder
\Sigma_{V}\times I\colon\Sigma_{V}\rightarrow\Sigma_{V}
, where the
\vect_{G}
-colored surface
\Sigma_{V}\cong\mathbb{D}^{2}
is a disk whose boundary is decorated with
n
positively oriented points colored by the objects
V_{i}
. As the cylinder
\Sigma_{V}\times I
represents the identity cobordism, we have
Z_{G}(\Sigma_{V}\times I)=\id_{Z_{G}(\Sigma_{V})}
.

The cobordism coming from the move
\Delta_{3}^{\alpha_{i}}
can be decomposed as
\Sigma_{V}\xrightarrow{E^{\alpha_{i}}}\emptyset\xrightarrow{\overline{E}^{\alpha_{i}^{*}}}\Sigma_{V}
, where
E^{\alpha_{i}}
and
\overline{E}^{\alpha_{i}^{*}}
had been introduced just before
Eq.

3.1
. We will prove the following two facts:

1.

There is a canonical isomorphism of vector spaces
Z_{G}(\Sigma_{V})\cong\vect_{G}\left(\C,V_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{n}\right)
.

2.

Under the identification above, the linear map
Z_{G}(E^{\alpha_{i}})\colon Z_{G}(\Sigma_{V})\rightarrow\C
is given by
f\mapsto(\alpha_{i}\circ f)(1)
.

First, we show how the proposition follows from the above claims. Gluing
\overline{E}^{\alpha_{i}^{*}}
and
E^{\alpha_{j}}
along
\Sigma_{V}
, we obtain a ball
B_{\Gamma(\alpha_{i}^{*},\alpha_{j})}
, where
\Gamma(\alpha_{i}^{*},\alpha_{j})
is the obvious graph with two vertices that can be evaluated as
\langle\Gamma(\alpha_{i}^{*},\alpha_{j})\rangle=\alpha_{j}\circ\alpha_{i}^{*}=\delta_{i,j}\id_{\C}
. Thus, by
Proposition

4.15
and the claimed identities, we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}\left(Z_{G}\Big(\overline{E}^{\alpha_{i}^{*}}\Big)(1)\right)\overset{\text{(i) and (ii)}}{=}Z_{G}\left(E^{\alpha_{i}}\cup_{\mathbb{D}^{2}}\overline{E}^{\alpha_{i}^{*}}\right)=Z_{G}\left(B_{\Gamma(\alpha_{i}^{*},\alpha_{j})}\right)\overset{\lx@cref{refnum}{P4}}{=}\delta_{i,j},

and thus
Z_{G}\left(\overline{E}^{\alpha_{i}^{*}}\right)=\alpha_{i}^{*}
. Hence, using
Eq.

2.2
, we can compute

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}Z_{G}\left(\overline{E}^{\alpha_{i}^{*}}\cup_{\emptyset}E^{\alpha_{i}}\right)(f)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}Z_{G}\left(\overline{E}^{\alpha_{i}^{*}}\right)\circ Z_{G}(E^{\alpha_{i}})(f)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}^{*}\circ\alpha_{i}\circ f=f,

and as
Z_{G}(\Sigma_{V}\times I)
is the identity, this proves the proposition.

Thus, what is left is to prove the above two claims. Applying
(-)_{c}\circ\Theta
, we obtain the cobordism
E_{i}=\left(E^{\alpha_{i}}\right)_{c}\colon\mathbb{S}_{V}\rightarrow\emptyset
. Here,
E_{i}
is a 3-ball, stratified as follows: All strata of codimension 1 and higher are contained in a closed disk embedded in such a way that removing it cuts the 3-ball into two disjoint pieces, each also homeomorphic to a 3-ball, one colored by
G
, the other by the trivial group. The only 0-stratum is a vertex labeled by
\alpha_{i}
in the center of the disk, where the
n
1-strata that are radii of the disk are attached, labeled by the objects
V_{i}
. The 2-strata are given by the complement of the 0- and 1-strata in the disk, each homeomorphic to a disk and labeled by the regular
G
-set. The sphere
\mathbb{S}_{V}
has the induced, orientation-reversed stratification.

The above describes a fine stratification (all strata are balls of the respective dimensions) and allows us to use
[
FMM
, Corollary 5.23]
to compute the gauge groupoid as
\mathcal{A}^{E_{i}}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathcal{G}^{E_{i}}\cong G^{P}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}G
, where
P=S_{2}^{E_{i}}
is the set of 2-strata. Noticing that
\iota_{1}\colon S_{1}^{\partial Ei}\hookrightarrow S_{2}^{E_{i}}
is a bijection, we obtain the same groupoid for the boundary gauge groupoid. By restriction of gauge data, it follows that the functor
P_{\partial}\colon\mathcal{A}^{E_{i}}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathcal{G}^{E_{i}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\partial E_{i}}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathcal{G}^{\partial E_{i}}
is the identity. We aim to compute the limit of

\displaystyle F_{\mathbb{S}_{V}}=F_{\overline{\partial E}_{i}}=\bigotimes_{v\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}S_{0}^{\overline{\partial E}_{i}}}F_{v}^{\overline{\partial E}_{i}}\colon\mathcal{A}^{\partial E_{i}}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathcal{G}^{\partial E_{i}}\rightarrow\vect_{\C},

since the TQFT assigns the space
Z_{G}(\Sigma_{V})=\lim F_{\mathbb{S}_{V}}
. The components
F_{v}^{\overline{\partial E}_{i}}
are given by

\displaystyle F_{v}^{\overline{\partial E}_{i}}\colon G^{S}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}G\xrightarrow{P_{v}}G\times G\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}G\simeq G\xrightarrow{\rho_{e}}\vect_{\C},

where
e
is the 1-stratum of
E_{i}
inducing the boundary stratum
v
(that is,
\iota_{0}(v)=e
), and
\oplus_{x\in G}\rho_{e}(x)
with
\rho_{e}(x)=(V_{j})_{x}
is the
G
-graded vector space
e
is labeled by. Using that the orientations on the boundary and on
\mathbb{S}_{V}
are flipped, we obtain

\displaystyle Z_{G}(\Sigma_{V})

\displaystyle=\lim F_{\mathbb{S}_{V}}=\bigoplus_{Gh\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}G^{P}/G}F_{\mathbb{S}_{V}}(h)^{\Aut(h)}=\bigoplus_{Gh\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}G^{P}/G}\bigotimes_{e\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}E}\rho_{e}(h_{\ell(e)}^{-1}h_{r(e)})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{g\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}\Omega_{w}}\bigotimes_{e\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}E}\rho_{e}(g_{e})\cong\vect_{G}(\C,V_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{n}),

(4.6)

where
w
is the sole vertex labeled by
\alpha_{i}
,
E=E(w)=S_{1}^{E_{i}}
and we reindexed through the set
\Omega_{w}=\big\{g\in G^{E}\,\big|\prod_{e\in E}g_{e}=1_{G}\big\}\subset G^{E}
and map
\varphi_{w}\colon G^{P}\rightarrow\Omega_{v}
from the proof of
Lemma

4.11
. The inverse map
\psi
of the isomorphism in
Section

4.2
simply maps a map
f
of
G
-graded vector spaces to its evaluation at
1\in\C
, or more precisely to
\sum_{Gh\in G^{P}/G}f_{\varphi_{w}(h)}(1)
, where we decompose into direct sum components. This shows the first statement (i).

With this preparation, we turn to the proof of (ii). First, note that the projection functor
P_{w}\colon\cala^{E_{i}}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\calg^{E_{i}}\rightarrow\cald_{w}
is the identity, where
w
is the unique vertex of
E_{i}
. The natural transformation
\mu_{E_{i}}=\langle\Gamma^{E_{i}}\rangle\colon F_{\mathbb{S}_{V}}\circ P_{\partial}\Rightarrow\C
therefore has components

\displaystyle\mu_{h}^{E_{i}}=(\sigma_{w}P_{w})_{h}=(\sigma_{w})_{h}=(\alpha_{i})_{\varphi_{w}(h)},

where
\sigma_{w}\colon\rho_{v}^{t}\Rightarrow\C
is the natural transformation derived from
\alpha_{i}
labeling the central vertex
w
, as in the proof of
Lemma

4.11
(here,
\sigma_{w}=\widetilde{\sigma}_{w}^{*}
). With this, we want to determine the map
L(s,\mu_{E_{i}})\colon\lim F_{\mathbb{S}_{V}}\rightarrow\C
from
Proposition

2.19
, where
s
denotes the span
\cala^{\partial E_{i}}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\calg^{\partial E_{i}}\leftarrow\cala^{E_{i}}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\calg^{E_{i}}\rightarrow\bullet
. Using that all fibers
\{h|\mathcal{A}^{E_{i}}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathcal{G}^{E_{i}}|\bullet\}
of the gauge groupoid are trivial and recalling notation from
Section

2.4.2
, we compute for
f\in\vect_{G}(\C,V_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{n})\mspace{1mu}
:

\displaystyle Z_{G}(E^{\alpha_{i}})(f)

\displaystyle=Z_{DW}(E_{i})(f)=(L(s,\mu_{E_{i}})\circ\psi)(f)=\sum_{[h]\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}\pi_{0}\left(\mathcal{A}^{\partial E_{i}}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathcal{G}^{\partial E_{i}}\right)}(\iota_{\bullet}\circ S_{\mu_{E_{i}}}^{h,\bullet}\circ\pi_{h}\circ\psi)(f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{Gh\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}G^{P}/G}\mu_{h}^{E_{i}}(f_{\varphi_{w}(h)}(1))=\sum_{Gh\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}G^{P}/G}(\alpha_{i})_{\varphi_{w}(h)}(f_{\varphi_{w}(h)}(1))

\displaystyle=\sum_{g\mspace{1mu}\in\mspace{1mu}\Omega_{v}}(\alpha_{i})_{g}(f_{g}(1))=(\alpha_{i}\circ f)(1).

This computation shows the second claimed identity and thus finishes the proof.
∎

The main result of this section now follows from the preceding propositions.

Theorem 4.17
.

Let
G
be a finite group and
Z_{G}\colon\Cob_{3}^{\vect_{G}}\rightarrow\vect_{\C}
boundary-defect Dijkgraaf-Witten theory. Then, the following are true:

1.

Z_{G}
is boundary local, that is, it satisfies conditions
1
–
4
from
Definition

3.2
.

2.

Z_{G}
is monoidally isomorphic to the Turaev-Viro boundary-defect TQFT
|\cdot|_{\vect_{G}}
for the spherical fusion category
\vect_{G}
.

Proof.

With the results of
Proposition

4.12
,
Proposition

4.14
,
Proposition

4.16
and
Proposition

4.15
, (i) follows, and
Theorem

3.22
for the spherical fusion category of
G
-graded vector spaces
\mathcal{C}=\vect_{G}
yields
Z_{G}\cong|\cdot|_{\vect_{G}}
and hence statement (ii).
∎

As a corollary, we have the following well-known result (for a different proof, see, for example,
[
TV
, Theorem H.1]
).

Corollary 4.18
.

The restrictions of
|\cdot|_{\vect_{G}}
and
Z_{G}
to the subcategory
\Cob_{3}
are isomorphic as monoidal functors, that is, Turaev-Viro theory for
\vect_{G}
without defects and untwisted Dijkgraaf-Witten theory for
G
without defects are equivalent.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
Theorem

4.17
and
Corollary

3.23
.
∎
